      34  ## What failed, and why
      35 
      36  **Haiku's two refusals were a bug in our band check.** Both cam
          e on "What if a hurricane counted double?". Haiku ran the what-
          if, labelled it, named Miami Very high (true in the what-if) an
          d cited the official ranking as well. The check kept one band p
          er hub, the last one cited, so it compared Very high with the o
          fficial Moderate and refused a right answer twice. It now accep
          ts any band a cited result gives the hub (`src/wra/agent/checks
          .py`, `_cited_bands`). With the fix, Haiku refused nothing.

  the guard rule (q19) and the two global lines (q20). The page check (q21) is

  - Paid check (q18): passed on 27c5b6d. The evaluation got 42 of 42, and the
    run cost about 0.05 USD. Committed in 8130f3f, with the run record.
  - Guard rule (q19): a line under Done / Dropped now has to say "Done <date>"
    or "dropped", or the commit is refused. Committed in 4bc1367, with the
    guard's first 7 tests. The two tests for the new rule failed before the
    change, and the full suite passes at 367. In a throwaway copy of the
    repository, filing an open item under Done was refused at commit, naming the
    item.
  - Global lines (q20): both are in ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md. The subagent line sits
    after the gh rule. The staging rule is folded into "Before any commit", so

    behind the public URL.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The guard compares the last commit's queue with the queue being committed,
    because the failure it exists for is a missing line. A dropped line leaves
    nothing behind to scan.
  - The new rule checks the item's own words, not just which section it sits in.
    A section heading only says where a line was filed, not whether the work is
    finished.
  - The test loads the hook from its file path because of the hyphen in
    queue-guard.py. Registering the module before loading it is what lets its
    dataclass work.

⏺ I'm running this conversation on Opus, as you chose. Your rules put designs on
  Fable, so I'll have a Fable reviewer read the written design before you see
  it. If you'd rather Fable lead the whole design, switch with /model fable and
  I'll carry on from here.

  What I understand you want: the chat page and its API on a public web address,
  so it can be used without your laptop. It needs a guard first, because every
  question spends your API credit. The assignment says "Deploying it to a URL is
  preferred, but not mandatory," and also that you must be able to run the
  agent live in the follow-up interview.

  What I found that bears on it:
  - On Haiku an answer costs about 0.075 cents on average, measured. The
    settings allow at most six model calls of up to 16,000 output tokens each
    per question. That puts the worst case near 5 cents, from output alone.
  - Conversations are kept in the server's memory, with no limit on how many.
  - The repository has no remote, so the code is on your machine only.
  - The database builds from committed files with no network. Any host can
    rebuild it at start-up.

  Things you may not be asking yet:
  - A door is not a cap. A passcode keeps strangers out, but a leaked passcode
    or a script stuck in a loop still spends credit. A daily limit on questions,
    in our code, bounds the worst case.
  - A separate API key for the host. You could switch it off without touching
    your own key. Anthropic may let you set a spending limit on it. That is
    UNVERIFIED, and I'll check before relying on it.
  - Where the host gets the code. Most hosts build from a GitHub repository;
    some upload from your machine. It depends on how you hand in the source.
  - The key leaves .env. A host keeps it in its own secret settings, which bends
    project rule 7. That needs your OK.
  - Free hosts fall asleep. The first question after an idle spell is slow, and
    every open conversation is lost.
  - Running locally stays the backup for the interview.

  Correct anything I've got wrong.

  My question: who do you picture opening this link, and when? The answer
  decides three things: whether the passcode is one you send the interviewers or
  one only you type, how long the site must stay up, and whether a slow first
  start would hurt.

❯ In the context of this assignment, the link is for analysts, daily. in the

⏺ That gives the link two jobs. Here is what I take from it.

  Who it's for, and what that means for the build

  - In the story, the analysts use it every day. So it should read as a daily
    tool: a sign-in step, then the chat. In a real company that step would be
    the firm's own sign-on. Here a simple stand-in does the job. (My proposal.)
  - In reality, the reviewers use it on their own, probably within a week, maybe
    later. You won't be there to restart it or explain it. Three proposals
    follow from that:
    a. One access code you send with your submission. The page asks for it once
       and remembers it in that browser. Without the code, no question reaches
       the model.
    b. A daily limit on questions, in our code. I'd pick 100. At the measured
       average that is about 7.5 cents a day. The worst case the settings allow
       is about 5 USD a day, from model output at full length on every question.
       When the limit is hit, the page says so plainly and makes no paid call.
    c. Always awake, with no end date. A reviewer's first question being slow,
       or failing, is the failure that matters most. So I'd avoid a free host
       that sleeps, even if it costs a small monthly fee. I'll check actual
       prices before proposing a host. It stays up until you take it down. If it
       crashes, the host restarts it, and the page already handles a lost
       conversation.

  What's wrong or missing there?

  Next question: how will you hand in the source code? For example a GitHub
  link, or a zip with your email. This decides how the host gets the code. It
  also decides whether the repository, including the session transcripts,
  becomes visible to anyone with the link.

  So the code goes to GitHub, and the host can build from there. Most hosts
  connect to a GitHub repository and rebuild when you push. That also lets the
  reviewers check that the code they read is the code that runs.

  Next question: should the repository be public or private?

  - Public. A link works for anyone, with no GitHub account or invitation
    needed. The code, the docs and every session transcript are visible to the
    world. The access code and the API key never go in the repository either
    way; they live in the host's secret settings.
  - Private. Only people you invite can see it. You'd need each reviewer's
    GitHub username, and you may not know who the reviewers are. The host can
    still build from it once you grant it access.

  I'd pick public. Your answer that you'll send "a GitHub link" suggests the
  reviewers expect to just click it. The scan shows nothing in the repository
  you'd need to hide. Did the company say anything about this, or is it your
  call?

› Message from general-purpose
  I read every page below on 2026-11-25. Quotes are copied word for word. "calc"
  marks my own arithmetic on a quoted rate, using a 30-day month. "Not found"
  means the official pages I read don't state it. I added no other hosts. The
  fetch tool cut off the Render and Cloud Run pricing pages, so I read those
  from the raw HTML with curl and wrote no files.

  **Render**
  - Free tier: "spins down a Free web service that goes 15 minutes without
  receiving any inbound traffic". Waking "takes about one minute"
  (render.com/docs/free). Credit card for the free tier: not found.
  - Always-on: paid plans "remove the Free instance limitations" (same page).
  The Hobby workspace is "$ 0 /mo + compute". The cheapest instance is "Less
  than 1 CPU $7/month 512 MB RAM 0.5c-512mb" (render.com/pricing), formerly
  called "Starter" (render.com/docs/compute-plans).
  - GitHub: "by default Render automatically rebuilds and redeploys", but "a
  public Git repository URL must be deployed manually", so link the GitHub
  account instead (render.com/docs/deploys). Native Python build with uv: "adds
  uv … if your project's root directory includes a uv.lock file"
  (render.com/docs/uv-version).
  - Secrets: "In the Render Dashboard, select the service you want to add an
  environment variable to" (render.com/docs/configure-environment-variables).
  - URL: "a unique onrender.com subdomain" (render.com/docs/web-services).
  - Request timeout: "HTTP responses to take up to 100 minutes"
  (render.com/docs/render-vs-vercel-comparison).

  **Fly.io**
  - Free tier: "$1 of credit with no credit card, and $5 of usage every month
  once you add a card". The same page also says "All organizations (except for
  Linked Organizations) require a credit card on file"
  (docs.fly.io/about/pricing).
  - Idle behaviour: `fly launch` sets `min_machines_running = 0` by default, and
  "A cold start takes about 2 seconds or more"
  (docs.fly.io/launch/autostop-autostart).
  - Always-on: set `min_machines_running` to 1. Prices are "shared-cpu-1x with
  256MB RAM | $2.19/month" and, for 512MB, "$3.69/month" (pricing). Either fits
  inside the $5 monthly usage, if that applies (calc).
  - GitHub: deploy on push needs a GitHub Actions workflow with a
  `FLY_API_TOKEN` secret
  (docs.fly.io/launch/continuous-deployment-with-github-actions). `fly launch`
  generates "a Dockerfile" (docs.fly.io/python/frameworks/fastapi).
  - Secrets: set with the `fly secrets set` command. Setting them in the
  dashboard: not found (docs.fly.io/apps/secrets).
  - URL: "https://[app-name].fly.dev/" (FastAPI guide above).
  - Risks for this app:
    - The `--ha` flag means "Create spare machines … (default true)"
  (docs.fly.io/flyctl/cmd/fly_launch). Use `--ha=false` to keep a single
  instance.
    - Request timeout: not found. The docs only show a configurable
  `idle_timeout` (docs.fly.io/reference/configuration). Unofficial forum posts
  report connections cut after 30 to 60 s idle.

  **Railway**
  - Free tier: "$1 of resource usage each month", which covers "100 MB, around
  the clock". Our app uses 120 MB, so that credit is too small. Free services
  "sleep after 5 to 10 minutes without outbound traffic"
  (docs.railway.com/reference/pricing/free-trial).
  - Waking: wake time not found. "The first request sent to a slept service may
  return a 502 Bad Gateway response" (docs.railway.com/reference/app-sleeping).
  - Always-on: Hobby is "$5 / month" and "includes $5 of resource usage per
  month" (docs.railway.com/reference/pricing/plans).
    - Memory costs "$10 per GB per month" (railway.com/pricing), about $1.20 for
  120 MB plus CPU (calc).
    - Sleeping is opt-in (the page refers to "how to enable Serverless",
  app-sleeping page).
    - Credit card: not found.
  - GitHub: "Automatically deploy when you push to GitHub"
  (docs.railway.com/deployments). Its native builder, Railpack, handles uv:
  "Uses pyproject.toml and uv.lock" (railpack.com/languages/python).
  - Secrets: "click on New Variable" (docs.railway.com/variables).
  - URL: "Get a .railway.app domain instantly"
  (docs.railway.com/reference/public-networking).
  - Request timeout: "up to 15 minutes if data keeps transferring", and "closed
  after 5 minutes with no data transferred"
  (docs.railway.com/networking/public-networking/specs-and-limits).
  - Risk: accounts left unverified on the trial get "restricted outbound network
  access" (free-trial page), which could block the Anthropic API calls.

  **Google Cloud Run** (paths below are under docs.cloud.google.com/run/docs/)
  - Free tier: "First 180,000 vCPU-seconds free per month"
  (cloud.google.com/run/pricing). Minimum instances default to "0", so it scales
  to zero (configuring/min-instances). Cold-start length: not found.
  - Always-on: there is no plan; you set minimum instances to 1.
    - The idle rate is "$0.0000025" per vCPU-second and per GiB-second
  (pricing).
    - That works out to about $4.86/month at 0.25 vCPU with 512 MiB, or about
  $9.72 at 1 vCPU, before the free tier (calc).
  - Single instance: setting max instances to 1 "can be exceeded for a brief
  period" (configuring/max-instances).
  - GitHub: a Cloud Build trigger deploys "whenever new commits are pushed", and
  Python builds without a Dockerfile (continuous-deployment). uv support: I
  didn't check.
  - Secrets: the console has a "Reference a secret" option
  (configuring/services/secrets).
  - URL: "All Cloud Run services have a stable HTTPS URL"
  (triggering/https-request).
  - Request timeout: "set by default to 5 minutes", and "up to 60 minutes"
  (configuring/request-timeout).
  - Credit card: "you must provide a credit card or other payment method"
  (docs.cloud.google.com/free/docs/free-cloud-features).

  **Hugging Face Docker Spaces** (paths below are under
  huggingface.co/docs/hub/)
  - Free tier: there is none for Docker any more. "Gradio and Docker Spaces run
  on compute and require a paid plan to create: PRO for personal accounts"
  (spaces-overview). PRO costs "$9 /month" (huggingface.co/pricing).
  - Idle behaviour: the basic CPU hardware sleeps "if inactive for more than a
  set time (currently, 48 hours)" (spaces-gpus). Wake time: not found.
  - Always-on: the CPU Upgrade hardware is "$22" monthly (pricing). With PRO
  that totals $31 (calc). "Upgraded Spaces run indefinitely by default"
  (spaces-gpus).
  - GitHub: deploy on push goes through the `huggingface/hub-sync` GitHub Action
  (spaces-github-actions). A Dockerfile is required (spaces-sdks-docker).
  - Secrets: "add a new variable or secret" in Settings (spaces-overview).
  - URL: `https://<space-subdomain>.hf.space` (spaces-overview).
  - Request timeout: not found.

  **Koyeb** (paths below are under koyeb.com/docs/)
  - Free tier: one 512MB instance, which will "scale down to zero when they
  don't receive any traffic for 1 hour" (reference/instances). This "cannot be
  disabled", and waking takes "1-5 seconds" (run-and-scale/scale-to-zero).
  - Always-on: the Starter plan is "pay-as-you-go" (faqs/pricing). Instance
  prices are `eco-nano` 256MB "$1.61" and `eco-micro` 512MB "$2.68" monthly
  (reference/instances).
  - Credit card: "We require a credit card", with a "$29 pre-authorization hold"
  (faqs/pricing).
  - GitHub: auto-deploy works for "repositories owned by your GitHub
  organization", building with a buildpack or a Dockerfile
  (build-and-deploy/deploy-with-git).
  - Secrets: added by "setting its value to an environment variable"
  (reference/secrets).
  - URL: a koyeb.app address with automatic TLS (reference/edge-network).
  - Request timeout: "The connection timeout for HTTP requests is set to 100
  seconds" (reference/edge-network).

  | Host | Free tier idle behaviour | Cheapest always-on, USD/month | GitHub
  auto-deploy | Request timeout | Notes |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Render | Sleeps after 15 min; about 1 min to wake | 0.5c-512mb (Starter), $7
  | Native | 100 min | Native uv build |
  | Fly.io | Stops when idle; about 2 s to wake | shared-cpu-1x, $2.19 (256MB)

  GitHub Action | Not found | Dockerfile required |
  | Koyeb | Scales to zero after 1 h; 1–5 s to wake | eco-nano $1.61 / eco-micro
  $2.68 | Native | 100 s | Card |

  **My opinion:** Render Starter ($7) and Railway Hobby ($5) fit best. Both
  deploy on push with native uv builds, take secrets in the dashboard, give an
  HTTPS subdomain, don't sleep on those plans, and allow far more than 90 s per
  request. Render's flat price is more predictable, while Railway bills by
  usage. Fly.io and Koyeb are cheaper, but Fly's request timeout isn't
  documented and Koyeb's 100 s limit leaves only 10 s above our slowest answer.

  Where it runs: three real options

  Host and plan: Render, 512 MB instance
  Per month: $7, flat
  What setting it up takes: Connect GitHub. It builds from our uv.lock by
    itself and redeploys on every push. The key and access code go in its
    dashboard.

    (my estimate).

    uv.lock, secrets go in its dashboard
  The catch: Billed by usage, so the bill is less predictable
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Host and plan: Fly.io, smallest machine
  Per month: $2.19 to $3.69
  What setting it up takes: A Dockerfile, a GitHub workflow holding a deploy
    token, and its command-line tool to set secrets
  The catch: More parts to build and maintain. By default it starts a spare

    first request after sleeping. Either one is a reviewer's first impression.
  - Koyeb. Its 100-second connection limit leaves only 10 seconds above our
    90-second limit per answer.
  - Google Cloud Run and Hugging Face. More setup or more cost than the three
    above, with nothing gained. I took that from the research and didn't recheck
    their pages.

  I'd pick Render. The site will stay up for an unknown length of time, and a
  flat $7 is a bill you can predict. It also needs nothing from us beyond a
  start command. Railway is the close second if saving $2 a month matters more
  than a predictable bill.

  What stays the same on any host (my calls, open to correction):
  - The door: the browser's own sign-in box, with a username and code you send
    the reviewers. It needs no change to the page, so it adds nothing to the
    page logic that only gets checked by hand. The alternative is a code field
    on the page itself. That looks more like a product, but it adds page logic
    that needs its own hand check.
  - The cap: 100 questions a day, counted by the server, plus a limit on how
    many conversations it holds in memory.
  - A separate Anthropic workspace and key, with its own monthly spending limit.
  - The health check: /healthz stays open, so the host can see the server is up
    without spending anything.

  Which host do you want? And if you'd rather have the code field on the page
  than the browser's box, say so.

⏺ Yes, there are free options. With "a week or two, maybe less", two of my
  premises change, so I rechecked them:

  - How long it stays up. I had assumed weeks to months. Render charges paid
    instances by the second, so its $7-a-month instance costs about $1.60 for a 
    week, $3.30 for two (my arithmetic). Its base plan is "$0/mo + compute".
  - What "slow" means. A wake-up of a few seconds is fine; a minute is not. Our
    server takes about 2 seconds to start, measured locally.
  - Koyeb's 100-second limit. I dropped Koyeb for this. But our code already
    ends every answer by 90 seconds, so the limit is never reached. I withdraw
    that objection.

  Option: Koyeb, free instance
  Two weeks costs: $0
  Card: Yes, a $29 hold they cancel straight away
  Sleeps when idle?: After 1 hour; this can't be turned off
  First question after idle: 1 to 5 s for Koyeb to wake (their figure), plus
    our ~2 s start
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: Fly.io, free allowance
  Two weeks costs: $0. The smallest always-on machine ($2.19 to $3.69 a month)
    fits inside the "$5 of usage every month once you add a card"
  Card: Yes
  Sleeps when idle?: No, if kept running
  First question after idle: No delay
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: Render, paid instance
  Two weeks costs: About $3.30
  Card: Probably. I haven't confirmed it
  Sleeps when idle?: No
  First question after idle: No delay
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: Render, free
  Two weeks costs: $0
  Card: Their free-tier page doesn't say
  Sleeps when idle?: After 15 minutes
  First question after idle: "about one minute"

  - Koyeb and Render connect to GitHub and build the app by themselves. Render's
    build-from-GitHub is confirmed on its own pages; Koyeb's is per the
    research.
  - Fly.io needs a Dockerfile, its command-line tool, and a setting to stop it
    starting a spare machine. Its time limit per request is undocumented.
  - Render free stays out. A reviewer waiting a minute for the first page is the
    failure we're designing against.
  - Every sleeping option resets conversations when it sleeps. The page already
    handles that.

  My pick: Koyeb free. It costs nothing, it's the least setup of the free
  options, and its few-seconds wake-up is acceptable for reviewers. Before the
  interview you'd open the page once so it's awake.

  If you'd rather spend about $3 to never think about sleep, Render paid is just
  as simple to set up.

  Which do you want?

› Message from general-purpose
  Four questions are left, ranked below. I read
  `/Users/doronreiffman/Projects/weather-risk-agent/docs/DEPLOY_DESIGN.md` and
  the code it cites, and ran read-only local checks (no network, no `gh`, `.env`
  untouched, nothing written). The design is still a draft, so I assumed its
  stated defaults stand at approval: public repository, the browser sign-in box,
  100 questions, 200 conversations, 5 USD.

  ## 1. What first-load wait after an idle hour passes, and what happens if the
  free instance misses it?

  The host choice rests on "1 to 5 s wake plus about 2 s start" (section 4), and
  the 2 s was measured on the laptop. Start-up is CPU work, so it stretches on
  a smaller CPU share.

  - **Measured now on this laptop** (Apple M2, three runs, no model call): 1.3
  to 1.6 s of CPU time before the app exists. About 1.0 s is imports and 0.26 s
  is loading the data and scoring. Peak memory was about 115 MB.
  - **Not in the design:** the free instance's CPU share. From memory, not
  checked today, it is a tenth of a core with 512 MB.
  - **If that is right:** start-up is roughly 15 to 30 s on top of Koyeb's wake.
  This is an estimate, not a measurement.
  - **Why the design does not settle it:** that range sits between goal 2's "a
  few seconds" and the one-minute wait that ruled out Render free. Section 5.6
  has the builder measure start time but sets no pass mark, and 5.10 has no wake
  check on the real URL.
  - **Also unverified:** that Koyeb holds the first request while the instance
  wakes instead of failing it, and for how long. A failed first request is the
  reason Railway was dropped.

  I need a pass mark in seconds and the fallback. I can measure in the image
  under a CPU limit before the first push: `docker` and `colima` are installed,
  though I did not check that colima is running. The fallbacks I can see:

  - accept the wait and say so in the README;
  - move to a host in the design's table that does not sleep, which means a
  different build and setup, so most of the plan changes;
  - keep it awake with a scheduled request to `/healthz`. Whether that postpones
  the sleep, and whether Koyeb allows it, is unchecked.

  ## 2. What exactly are the history scan's terms, where do they live, and what
  is the remedy for a hit?

  Section 5.7 names categories (work email domain, employer's and colleagues'
  names, work project names) but gives no list and no place to keep one. Three
  things change the plan:

  - **The list.** Only the domain can be derived, from the machine-wide git
  config. The names cannot, and a builder cannot know a guessed list is
  complete. This is a one-way door.
  - **Keeping the terms out of transcripts.** A term typed into a scan command
  lands in a session transcript, and transcripts are committed to the public
  repository. Section 5.7 says session 06 already holds one such name, in a
  question Claude wrote. So the plan needs the list in a file outside the
  repository, commands that print only counts and locations, and the same scan
  before every later transcript push. I need the owner to confirm that and say
  who writes the file.
  - **The remedy.** Rewriting history changes every later commit id. `README.md`
  cites `27c5b6d` as proof, and `.claude/QUEUE.md` cites twenty ids. So
  "rewrite" and "publish a fresh history" are very different plans.

  I ran the part that can be derived, across all 74 commits, and it is clean:

  | Search | Lines found |
  |---|---|
  | Work domain in patches | 0 |
  | Work domain in commit messages | 0 |
  | Author or committer emails on the work domain (one distinct author email in
  all) | 0 |
  | The domain's first label, as the employer's name | 0 |
  | claude.ai session links | 0 |
  | `sk-ant-` | 3, all the `QUEUE.md` line quoting the search text, with no key
  characters after it |

  Only the names that cannot be derived remain unscanned.

  ## 3. Which GitHub account owns the public repository, and is `gh` on this
  machine signed in as it?

  Section 5.7 says only "done in the main session with `gh`". The project
  `CLAUDE.md` says the machine-wide git identity is the work address, so `gh`
  may be signed in to a work-linked account. I was not allowed to run `gh`, so I
  could not check. The repository has no remote, and no tracked file names an
  account.

  - If the active account is the wrong one, the plan needs an account switch and
  push credentials for the right one before the repository is created.
  - Koyeb's GitHub connection is granted on whichever account owns the
  repository.
  - A repository created under the wrong account is public under that name at
  once.

  ## 4. Does the owner's acceptance of the station data's unconfirmed terms
  extend to a public repository?

  `/Users/doronreiffman/Projects/weather-risk-agent/docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md:120`
  (open point O1) records that the owner accepted committing the ACIS raw
  responses "to this repository" on 2026-11-24, with the service's terms still
  unconfirmed. The repository had no remote then. Going public republishes those
  files permanently, and the design does not revisit the point.

  A "yes" changes nothing. A "no" means removing `data/raw/acis/` from the files
  and the history. That breaks the offline build the image and the tests rely
  on, so it must be known before the first push.

  Answer by number.

  1. The wake-up wait for reviewers. Our start-up takes about 1.5 seconds of a

  core, the first page after an idle hour would likely take 15 to 30 seconds.
  That's an estimate, not measured on Koyeb, and Koyeb's pages don't say whether

  - (a) Stay on Koyeb free. Measure the real wait after the first deploy, and

    seconds").

    ever waits. The code changes are the same.

  I'd pick (b), because the reason Koyeb won was "a few seconds", and that
  likely doesn't hold. Since you said interview day is what matters, (a) is a
  fair choice too.

  2. Which GitHub account. I'd create the repository under your personal account
  (doronreiffman). I'd switch gh to it only for the creation and the first

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've written the build plan and committed it as 5298304. It's at
  docs/DEPLOY_PLAN.md, 191 lines. Please review it.

  The six tasks:
  1. The agent's two limits. At most 100 questions a day, switched on only by
     the public server; on a new day the count starts again. At most 200
     conversations held, both locally and online.
  2. Public mode and the sign-in. The server refuses to start on a public
     address without the access code. The browser's sign-in box guards
     everything except the health check.
  3. The build file for Koyeb. It gets tried on your laptop at a tenth of a
     processor core. That gives you a realistic wake-up time before anything
     goes public. Docker runs on this machine.
  4. Documents, including the reworded rule 7. Then a fresh Fable reviewer reads
     the whole change before anything goes public.
  5. GitHub. The final scan, then switching gh to your personal account,
     creating the repository and pushing, and switching back. Each step waits
     for your go.
  6. Koyeb. You create the account, the service and the demo key, following my
     steps. I run the free checks; you run the browser checks. Then the wake-up
     wait gets measured twice on the real site.

  One refinement of the design. The design put the daily limit outside the
  agent. The plan has the server give it to the agent instead. A refused
  question on a brand-new conversation must still carry a real conversation id,
  because the page keeps whatever id a reply carries, and only the agent can
  make one. Your goal is unchanged: wra ask, wra chat and wra eval never meet
  the limit.

  How to run it:
  - Subagent-driven: a fresh agent builds each task, and a fresh reviewer checks
    it before the next starts. Most thorough, and most expensive.
  - Native: I build every task myself, then one Fable reviewer checks the whole
    change. Cheaper and faster.

  I recommend native. The four build tasks are small and each uses the one
  before it. The one costly mistake, putting something wrong on a public site,
  is guarded by the Fable review that comes before the push.

  I also suggest running it in a fresh session, since this one is long:
  1. Say "handoff", and I'll write the handoff and this session's index line.
  2. Run /export to docs/ai-session/06-closeout-and-design.txt. I'll reword the
     work-project line in it before it's committed.
  3. Run /clear. Opus is the right model for the build.

  Does the plan capture what you want, and which way should we run it?

       4 `wra eval` never meet it. The count lives in memory: it starts a
         gain on a new UTC day, and also whenever the
       5 process restarts, which on the free host is after every idle hou
         r. The hard ceiling is the demo workspace's
       6 monthly spending limit at Anthropic.
     … +29 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 6 lines
      35 
      36  `wra serve` listens on this machine only (127.0.0.1, port 8000)
          ; `--host` and `--port` change that. The page sends `POST /api/
          chat` with `{"session_id": null, "message": "..."}` and gets ba
          ck the validated reply; sending that reply's `session_id` with
          the next message makes it a follow-up. Every reply has the same
           shape. Its HTTP status is 200 unless it is an error: 409 while
           the conversation's last question is still being answered, 404
          for a conversation the server no longer holds (after a restart)
          , 502 when the model could not give a validated answer, 500 for
           a bug in the agent's own code. A malformed request gets 422 wi
          th FastAPI's validation message instead of a reply. `GET /api/h
          ubs` lists the hubs and `GET /healthz` checks the server; neith
          er calls the model. FastAPI's generated reference for the API i
          s at `/docs`.
      37 
      38 +### Public mode                                                
      39 +                                                               
      40 +`wra serve` on any address but 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1 runs
         + in public mode, the way the public demo runs. It needs an acce
         +ss code in `WRA_ACCESS_CODE` and refuses to start without one (
         +exit status 2). Every route but `/healthz` then asks for sign-i
         +n in the browser's own box: the username `analyst` and the acce
         +ss code as the password. At most 100 questions a day (UTC) reac
         +h the model; past that a question gets a plain message, HTTP 42
         +9 and no model call. In either mode the server holds at most 20
         +0 conversations and drops the one used least recently. Both lim
         +its are in `config/agent.toml`. The daily count is kept in memo
         +ry and starts again whenever the server restarts, so run the pu
         +blic site with a key from a separate Anthropic workspace that h
         +as its own monthly spending limit: that limit is the ceiling on
         + spend.

      103  **Decision:** For a comparison across several hazards, the fin
           al answer states each hazard's order in `hazard_orders`, which
            code checks against the cited result (one repair if wrong), a
           nd the comparison table carries a code-written note saying whi
           ch hub leads each hazard.
      104  **Why:** The final review showed a swapped comparison ("Housto
           n leads on hurricanes at 1.52") passes the number checks, sinc
           e every figure is still in the result. A grader reading the pa
           iring from word positions in the prose was built and then drop
           ped at the handoff review: a right answer phrased "Houston's h
           urricane exposure is lower than Miami's" would fail it, and an
           y word rule moves the misses elsewhere. The owner chose the st
           ructured claim plus the code note. Rejected: loosening the wor
           d rule; recording the limit only; a model judging the answer (
           the plan rules out a model grading a model). Same pattern as `
           hubs_ranked`: the remaining gap is prose that contradicts its
           own structured claim, and the code note keeps the true fact on

          +hange to the page, whose logic is checked only by hand; a code

          +n first said, because a refusal on a new conversation must car
          +ry a conversation id the agent holds (the page keeps the id an
          + error reply carries); only `wra serve` in public mode hands i
          +t over, so `wra ask`, `wra chat` and `wra eval` never meet it.
          + The count lives in memory and starts again on each restart, s
          +o the hard ceiling is a separate Anthropic workspace with its 
          +own monthly limit. Host (owner's choice): Koyeb's free instanc
          +e, over Fly.io's free allowance (more setup, a spare machine b

  ### Strengths

  - **The door holds.** 60-odd unauthenticated probes all got 401 (or 400/404),
  with 0 model calls: forged `Host` headers (`x/healthz?`, `x/healthz#` and
  seven variants, on `/api/hubs` and `POST /api/chat`), path tricks
  (`/healthz/../api/hubs`, `//healthz`, `/healthz/`, absolute-form targets),
  `/docs`, `/redoc`, `/openapi.json`, `OPTIONS`, `HEAD`. Only `/healthz` (and
  `/healthz?x=1`) is open.
  - **It fails closed, end to end.** The built image exits with status 2 and the
  error line when `WRA_ACCESS_CODE` is unset, empty, or three spaces. There is
  no importable `app` object without the door, so nothing but `wra serve` can
  start the site.
  - **The five Review Focus points all pass** (details below).
  - **No secret in the image.** `/app` holds no `.env`, `.git`, `.claude`,
  `.remember`, `.superpowers`, `docs/ai-session`, `evals/runs` or `tests`; the
  image's environment has only Python and uv variables.
  - **Tests check behaviour, not mocks.** The serve test's rework (a day's limit
  of 0, so local answers and public gets 429) is stronger than the plan's
  version that recorded keyword arguments. Test counts in the four commit
  messages add up (367 → 373 → 387 → 388).
  - **The plan's one refinement of the design is sound.** The limit sits in the
  agent because the page keeps the id an error reply carries
  (`src/wra/api/static/index.html:254`); I confirmed that line.
  - **A cross-site request cannot ride a signed-in browser:** with good
  credentials, a `text/plain`, form-encoded or untyped body gets 422 and no
  model call; the preflight gets 401.

  Review Focus, each checked:
  1. Limit reached on a new conversation's first question: the 429 reply's id is
  held (unit test, and over HTTP: a follow-up on that id got 429 `daily_limit`,
  not 404). Pass.
  2. Malformed sign-in headers: 18 shapes over real HTTP (no value, not base64,
  missing padding, inner space, no colon, not UTF-8, a Latin-1 pair, raw
  non-ASCII bytes, NUL, 60 kB of junk) all 401, none 500. Pass.
  3. Code `pa:ss-ü` (colon, non-ASCII): 200 with the right pair, also with a
  lowercase scheme and two spaces. Pass.
  4. Spaces as the code: `access_code_from_env()` gives None, and the image
  exits 2. Pass.
  5. Refusals do not use the count: with a limit of 3, unknown-conversation
  (404) and malformed (422) requests left the model-call count at 3; busy is
  covered by the unit test; `admit` is called only after both refusals
  (`src/wra/agent/loop.py:155`). Pass.

  ### Issues

  #### Critical (Must Fix)

  None.

  #### Important (Should Fix)

  1. **The documents say the site is deployed; it is not.** `docs/PLAN.md:675`
  ("Deployed as designed ..."; also says the guard "is met by ... a separate
  workspace with its own spending limit", an owner step not done yet),
  `docs/DECISIONS.md:109` ("the site runs on a Koyeb free instance"),
  `README.md:40` and `:42`, `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:112` ("The public site runs in
  the image ..."). Task 5 makes these public before Task 6 makes them true, and
  the host is still open to change. Fix: reword now to what exists ("built
  for", "will run"), or hold these sentences for Task 6 step 6.
  2. **Plan gap for Task 5: the work is on `public-url`; `main` is still at
  5298304.** Task 5 step 4 creates the repository and pushes from the current
  checkout, then expects `main` on the remote, and Koyeb builds `main`. The plan
  has no merge step (the Setup deviation added the branch). Fix: fast-forward
  `main` to the reviewed commit and check it out before step 4. How `gh repo
  create --push` treats the current branch is from my memory, not checked (no
  `gh` in this review).

  #### Minor (Nice to Have)

  1. `src/wra/api/public.py:54`: the door compares `request.url.path`, which
  Starlette rebuilds using the `Host` header, while routing uses
  `scope["path"]`. Safe on the locked Starlette 1.10.0 (it validates the header;
  probes above). `pyproject.toml` has lower bounds only. Compare
  `request.scope["path"]`, and add one test with `headers={"Host":
  "x/healthz?"}` so a dependency change cannot open the door.
  2. `Dockerfile:17`: only 14 of our 51 modules are byte-compiled in the image
  (those `wra ingest` imports); the project is installed as a path link to
  `/app/src`. Design 5.6 wanted no first-run work after a wake. Measured effect
  at 0.1 CPU: none I can distinguish (as shipped 38.6, 42.4, 41.8 s; precompiled
  36.9, 43.3 s). Optional: add `&& .venv/bin/python -m compileall -q src`.
  3. `src/wra/cli.py:210`: the code is read before `.env` is loaded
  (`loop.py:67`), so a code put in `.env` is ignored and the error says "is not
  set" (run: exit 2). Safe, but `README.md:40` and the message could say "in the
  environment, not `.env`".
  4. `src/wra/api/app.py:62-67`: the generated API reference lists 404, 409,
  502, 500 but not 429.
  5. `README.md`: the `wra rank` and `wra ingest --refresh` paragraphs now sit
  under "### Interview day".
  6. `docs/DECISIONS.md:110`: "a spare machine by default that would split
  conversations" (Fly.io) goes beyond the design's quoted text. Verify or mark
  it.
  7. `src/wra/api/app.py:60`: `create_app(..., access_code="")` builds an open
  app without complaint. Only `_serve` guards it today.
  8. `.dockerignore:3-4`: `.env` patterns match the root folder only;
  `.gitignore` matches any depth. No nested file exists, and Koyeb builds from
  git.
  9. `src/wra/agent/loop.py:147-150`: once the limit is used up, each refused
  first question still adds a held, empty conversation; 200 of them would push
  out real ones. A consequence of Focus 1; no action at this grade.
  10. A missing `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` in public mode still starts healthy, then
  every question is a 502. Task 6 step 4 catches it at deploy; a start-up
  refusal would be optional.
  11. Small wording left behind: `cli.py:69-70` ("until the server stops",
  "Needs ANTHROPIC_API_KEY in .env"), README's "404 ... (after a restart)". The
  plan's Deviations omit the serve test's change of method.

  ### Recommendations (for Tasks 5 and 6)

  - **Tell reviewers the username.** The design and plan say to send "the URL
  and the access code"; the browser's box also asks for a username, and not
  every browser shows the realm text. Put `analyst` in the message and in README
  "Public demo".
  - **Pick a long random ASCII code.** Nothing limits sign-in attempts, so the
  code's strength is the only barrier (spend is still capped by the workspace
  limit). ASCII avoids any doubt about how a browser encodes `ü`.
  - **Add three checks to Task 6** (Koyeb's behaviour is unverified here): the
  health check's grace period against a start of about 40 s; `curl -sI
  http://<URL>/` redirects to https before any sign-in; `curl -H 'Host:
  x/healthz?' <URL>/api/hubs` is not 200.

  Executor's rulings, weighed: the branch instead of a worktree is fine but
  caused Important 2. Dropping the Dockerfile text test is right; building and
  running the image is the real check, and I repeated it (exit 2 three ways, 401
  and 200, `PORT=9000` honoured, about 105 MB at rest). The measured start
  ("41.7 to 43.7 s") is consistent with my three runs.

  ### Declined to judge

  - The full-history scan for names and secrets: I scanned this range only; Task
  5 step 1 owns the rest.
  - Koyeb itself (holding a request during wake, health-check timing, https
  redirect, what its edge does with an odd `Host`): no network here; Task 6.
  - Flooding the site with unauthenticated requests at 0.1 CPU: the design's
  non-goals exclude monitoring and production hardening.
  - The count restarting after each sleep, so more than 100 a day is possible:
  accepted in design 5.3.
  - The roughly 40 s start against goal 2: the owner's recorded decision,
  tracked in `.claude/OPEN_ISSUES.md`.
  - The container running as root: demo grade; no secret in the image and the
  app writes nothing.
  - A conversation dropped while it is mid-answer: needs 200 new conversations
  inside one 90-second answer.
  - `max_conversations` or `daily_question_limit` set to 0 or below: the owner's
  config, no validation asked for.
  - `--host 0.0.0.0` on a local network over plain HTTP, and a 127.0.0.1 server
  put behind a tunnel: both outside the approved "mode by listen address"
  design.
  - An access code in the environment that is not valid UTF-8: would give 500 on
  a well-formed sign-in (still closed); not a realistic input.

  ### Assessment

  **Ready to merge?** With fixes.

  **Reasoning:** The code is sound and the two things that matter most hold
  under direct testing: no secret reaches the repository range or the image, and
  nothing reaches the model without the sign-in. Before Task 5, fix the
  documents' "deployed" wording and get the reviewed commit onto `main`; the
  Minor items can follow or be declined.

      35 
      36  `wra serve` listens on this machine only (127.0.0.1, port 8000)
          ; `--host` and `--port` change that. The page sends `POST /api/
          chat` with `{"session_id": null, "message": "..."}` and gets ba
          ck the validated reply; sending that reply's `session_id` with
          the next message makes it a follow-up. Every reply has the same
           shape. Its HTTP status is 200 unless it is an error: 409 while
           the conversation's last question is still being answered, 404
          for a conversation the server no longer holds (after a restart)
          , 502 when the model could not give a validated answer, 500 for
           a bug in the agent's own code. A malformed request gets 422 wi
          th FastAPI's validation message instead of a reply. `GET /api/h
          ubs` lists the hubs and `GET /healthz` checks the server; neith
          er calls the model. FastAPI's generated reference for the API i
          s at `/docs`.

✻ Churned for 2m 38s · done 3:43 AM

※ recap: Goal: put the agent on a public Koyeb site for the interview. The code is built, reviewed and live on GitHub, so next you create the Anthropic demo key and the Koyeb service, then send me the site address.

❯ before we do this - explain why we you gave me the options you did: Koyeb,    
  etc. why not a simpler hosting solution?                                      

⏺ Short answer: the shortlist came from what this app needs in order to run, and
  that rules out the simplest kinds of hosting. But I picked the six candidates
  myself, and I left out two options that really are simpler.

  What the app needs from a host
  - A program that stays running. It's a server that keeps each conversation in
    its own memory. Follow-up questions depend on that.
  - Time. An answer can take up to 90 seconds.
  - A secret. It calls Anthropic with a key the host has to hold.

  What that rules out
  - Static hosts (GitHub Pages, for example) only serve files; they can't run
    our Python at all.
  - Serverless hosts (Vercel, Netlify's functions, AWS Lambda) start a fresh
    copy for each request and stop it afterwards. Conversations would be
    forgotten between questions, and they cap how long a request may run. That's
    from memory, UNVERIFIED in detail; I can check if it matters.

  That left the "run my server" hosts, which is where the six came from.

  What I should have shown you as well
  - A tunnel from your laptop. Tools like ngrok or Cloudflare Tunnel give wra 
    serve on your laptop a public address with one command. There's no host
    account and no Dockerfile. The catch: it only works while your laptop is on
    and running it, so reviewers couldn't try it on their own time. (UNVERIFIED
    in detail.)
  - Not deploying at all. The assignment calls a URL "preferred, but not
    mandatory". You already run it locally in the interview, and that's proven.

  What doesn't depend on the host
  The sign-in and the daily cap are needed for any public address, a tunnel
  included, because every question spends your credit. That's most of what we
  built. Only the Dockerfile is host-specific. Render's paid plan builds
  straight from uv.lock, which I checked on its own pages, so it wouldn't have
  needed one.

  So the real choice is:

  ┌───────────────┬───────────┬────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │    Option     │   Cost    │ Reviewers can  │         Work left         │
  │               │           │  try it alone  │                           │
  ├───────────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Koyeb free    │           │ Yes, after a   │ The two browser steps I   │
  │ (current      │ $0        │ ~40 s wake     │ gave you                  │
  │ plan)         │           │                │                           │
  ├───────────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │               │ ~$3.30    │                │ About the same steps; the │
  │ Render paid   │ for two   │ Yes, no wait   │  Dockerfile works there   │
  │               │ weeks     │                │ too, or isn't needed      │
  ├───────────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Tunnel from   │           │ Only while     │ Install the tool, one     │
  │ your laptop   │ $0        │ your laptop    │ command                   │
  │               │           │ runs it        │                           │
  ├───────────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ No deployment │ $0        │ No             │ None                      │
  └───────────────┴───────────┴────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  My view: if reviewers trying it before the interview matters to you, a hosted
  option is the only one that delivers that. Of the two, Render paid is simpler
  to live with (no wake-up wait) for about $3. If only the interview matters,
  you don't need any of this; the local run covers it.

  Which do you want: carry on with Koyeb, switch to Render, or stop here?

  Switch to Render, or stay with Koyeb?

      109  **Decision:** `wra serve` on any address but this machine's ow
           n is public mode: it refuses to start without `WRA_ACCESS_CODE
           `, puts HTTP Basic sign-in in front of every route but `/healt
           hz`, and gives the agent a limit of 100 questions a UTC day; t
           he site runs on a Koyeb free instance from a Dockerfile.
      110  **Why:** A public URL spends the owner's API credit on every m
           essage, and the reviewers may use it alone. Tying the mode to
           the address means a deploy with a missing secret fails instead
            of opening the site. The browser's own sign-in box needs no c
           hange to the page, whose logic is checked only by hand; a code
            field on the page was rejected for that reason. The limit is
           handed to the agent, not checked in the API layer as the desig
           n first said, because a refusal on a new conversation must car
           ry a conversation id the agent holds (the page keeps the id an
            error reply carries); only `wra serve` in public mode hands i
           t over, so `wra ask`, `wra chat` and `wra eval` never meet it.
            The count lives in memory and starts again on each restart, s
           o the hard ceiling is a separate Anthropic workspace with its
           own monthly limit. Host (owner's choice): Koyeb's free instanc
           e, over Fly.io's free allowance (more setup, a spare machine b
           y default that would split conversations), Render paid (about
           $3.30 for two weeks, never sleeps) and Render free (about a mi
           nute to wake). Its 0.1 of a CPU makes a start after an idle ho
           ur slow (about 42 seconds measured locally at that size); the
           owner re-evaluates once it is measured on Koyeb.
      111  **Context:** `docs/DEPLOY_DESIGN.md`, `docs/DEPLOY_PLAN.md`; `
           src/wra/api/public.py`, `src/wra/agent/limits.py`, `src/wra/ag
           ent/loop.py`, `src/wra/cli.py` (`_serve`), `Dockerfile`, `.doc
           kerignore`.

  Over to you for the two deploy steps. They happen in your browser, and I don't
  drive it.

  Step 1: the demo key, in the Anthropic Console (platform.claude.com)
  1. Create a new workspace just for the demo, for example wra-demo.
  2. Open the Console's Spend limits page. Under the workspace limits, pick
     wra-demo and set a monthly limit of 5 USD. If that option isn't there, tell
     me before going on.
  3. Create an API key inside wra-demo, and keep it ready for step 2.

  Step 2: the service, on Render (render.com)
  1. Sign up, then connect GitHub with your personal account, granting access to
     weather-risk-agent only.
  2. Create a new Web Service from that repository, branch main.
  3. Runtime: Docker. It finds our Dockerfile.
  4. Instance: the $7-a-month, 512 MB one. Render may label it "Starter". It's
     billed by the second, so two weeks is about $3.30. It will probably ask for
     a card; I haven't confirmed that.
  5. Health check path: /healthz. Leave the port alone: Render tells the server
     which port to use, and the Dockerfile already reads it.
  6. Add two environment variables:
     - ANTHROPIC_API_KEY: the demo key from step 1.
     - WRA_ACCESS_CODE: a long random code. Make it in a terminal outside this
       session (openssl rand -hex 20) and keep it in your password manager.
       Don't paste it here or run it with !, or it ends up in the transcript.
  7. Deploy. The first build takes a few minutes. When the service shows as
     live, send me its address, and only the address.

  Then I run the free checks:
  - the health check answers;
  - the page asks for sign-in;
  - plain http redirects to https;
  - a forged header is refused.

  After that you sign in and ask one question, which costs under a cent.

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 5 lines, removed 1 line
      43 
      44  `wra serve` on any address but 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1 runs
           in public mode, which is how the public site is built to run.
          It needs an access code in `WRA_ACCESS_CODE` and refuses to sta
          rt without one (exit status 2). Every route but `/healthz` then
           asks for sign-in in the browser's own box: the username `analy
          st` and the access code as the password. At most 100 questions
          a day (UTC) reach the model; past that a question gets a plain
          message, HTTP 429 and no model call. In either mode the server
          holds at most 200 conversations and drops the one used least re
          cently. Both limits are in `config/agent.toml`. The daily count
           is kept in memory and starts again whenever the server restart
          s, so run the public site with a key from a separate Anthropic
          workspace that has its own monthly spending limit: that limit i
          s the ceiling on spend.

         -Y_DESIGN.md).

  Against "clarity, reasoning and thoughtful design; narrow scope done well"
  - Strong: the scope is narrow and every choice is argued. Twelve hubs, one
    measure, numbers only from code, and a confidence rating that says when two
    hubs can't really be told apart. The decision log and the transcripts show
    the reasoning. That's exactly what the line asks for.
  - The risk is volume. The documents total about 36,000 words. The planning doc
    alone is nearly 11,000, and the deployment design and plan add about 7,000
    for what the brief calls optional. The README's status paragraph is a single
    220-word block. A reviewer with half an hour may not find the core fast,
    and that costs "clarity".

         +cted answers stay fixed. Raw warnings are stored and the time u

      97  ## Contracts between the model and the code
      98 
      99  **Tool arguments.** `src/wra/agent/tooldefs.py` generates each
          tool's JSON schema from its Pydantic model and sends it with `s
          trict: true`, which makes the API guarantee the arguments match
          . Strict mode supports only part of JSON Schema, so numeric bou
          nds, length limits and patterns are dropped and enforced by Pyd
          antic in `run_tool`. Hub fields become an enumeration of the tw
          elve ids, so the model cannot name a city with no hub.

      69  - **Fourteen cases is a regression gate, not a fine instrument.
          ** At 42 case runs and a 90% pass rate, the usual 95% margin is
           about plus or minus 9 points, and repeats of the same question
           are not independent, so the real margin is wider.
      70  - **The number check confirms that each figure is in a cited re
          sult, not which hub or hazard it belongs to.** A right figure f
          or the wrong hub or hazard passes it: "Houston leads on hurrica
          nes at 1.52" would pass, since 1.52 is in the result as Miami's
           figure (final review, run offline on a scripted model). A wron
          g small whole number can match a rank or a count, and a claim i
          n words goes unchecked. No saved answer swapped figures. The an
          swer now states each hazard's order in a field code checks agai
          nst the cited result, the Miami and Houston case grades that fi
          eld, and a code-written note under the comparison table says wh
          ich hub leads each hazard. The tables beside each verdict alway
          s show the computed figures.
      71 -- **A claim in words can be false.** The Dallas answer below sa
         -ys winter drives its risk "mainly under winter storm, ice storm
         - and blizzard warnings". That is wrong: Dallas had 6 Winter Sto
         -rm Warnings, 1 Ice Storm Warning and no Blizzard Warning in 201
         -6 to 2025. No tool result splits winter by warning type; the mo
         -del took the list from the score's description, which names eve
         -ry type the score counts. The system prompt now tells the model
         - to name warning types only when a result lists them for that h
         -ub (final review).

         -b6d`: a fresh clone built offline, 360 tests, six questions thr

      54  `wra serve` on any address but 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1 runs
           in public mode, which is how the public site is built to run.
          It needs an access code in `WRA_ACCESS_CODE` and refuses to sta
          rt without one (exit status 2). Every route but `/healthz` then
           asks for sign-in in the browser's own box: the username `analy
          st` and the access code as the password. At most 100 questions
          a day (UTC) reach the model; past that a question gets a plain
          message, HTTP 429 and no model call. In either mode the server
          holds at most 200 conversations and drops the one used least re
          cently. Both limits are in `config/agent.toml`. The daily count
           is kept in memory and starts again whenever the server restart
          s, so run the public site with a key from a separate Anthropic
          workspace that has its own monthly spending limit: that limit i
          s the ceiling on spend.

  I reviewed /tmp/handoff-review.diff in two parts. Part 1 is the queue-guard
  rule (q19). Part 2 is the sign-in change and the Koyeb-to-Render comments. I
  read each changed file in full, plus the real queue, the locked Starlette
  source in .venv, app.py, the hook wrapper, .gitignore and .dockerignore,
  scripts/e2e.sh and DEPLOY_DESIGN.md sections 5.6 and 9. I could not run
  anything, so the "367 passed" and "failed before the change" claims in q19's
  queue line are not verified here.

  **No critical or important issues.** All findings are minor.

  ## Part 1: queue guard (q19)

  **Does it do what the owner asked?** Yes.
  - `CLOSED_PATTERN` plus the new loop in `check()` refuses a line under Done /
  Dropped unless it contains "Done YYYY-MM-DD" or "dropped" (case does not
  matter). That is the owner's wording, read literally.
  - The session-05 slip was q17, an open "Session 05 transcript committed" line
  filed under Done (docs/ai-session/05-results.txt:3468). A line without a date
  or "dropped" is now refused.
  - Of the two tests for the new rule,
  `test_an_open_item_filed_under_done_is_refused` and
  `test_done_without_a_date_is_refused`, I traced both through the old
  `check()`: each returns `[]` there, so both would fail on the old code. The
  other five pin behaviour the guard already had. There are 7 tests, matching
  the queue line.
  - The tests check real behaviour: `check()` is a pure function, and nothing is
  mocked or touches the network.
  - The comment about registering the module in `sys.modules` before loading it
  is correct: a dataclass in a module with `from __future__ import annotations`
  looks its module up there.

  **The real queue:** I checked every line under `## Done / Dropped` in
  /Users/doronreiffman/Projects/weather-risk-agent/.claude/QUEUE.md (lines 15 to
  34; 20 lines: q10, q21, q19, q20, q18, q17, q6, q13, q16, q15, q14, q5, q12,
  q4, q3, q11, q2, q9, q1, q7). Each contains "Done 2026-11-24" or "Done
  2026-11-25", so all pass. Ids q1 to q23 each appear exactly once, so the
  duplicate check passes too.

  **Finding P1-1 (minor, medium confidence):** /Users/doronreiffman/Projects/wea
  ther-risk-agent/.claude/hooks/queue-guard.py:27
  - The pattern matches anywhere in the line. A misfiled open item whose own
  description happens to say "dropped" would still pass, and so would one that
  quotes another item's "Done <date>".
  - The q19 line itself would pass on its quoted `"dropped"` even without its
  date.
  - This is the owner's rule taken literally, and it catches the real failure.
  At demo grade I would leave it. If tightened, require the marker after a
  sentence break; that is probably more than the task needs.

  **Verdict:** does the task, stays in scope, fits demo grade.

  ## Part 2(a): the sign-in check (src/wra/api/public.py:54-56,
  tests/test_public.py:78-85)

  **The fix is right.**
  - Starlette's router matches on `get_route_path(scope)`
  (.venv/.../starlette/_utils.py:137). That equals `scope["path"]` whenever
  `root_path` is unset.
  - Nothing in `src/` sets `root_path`, mounts a sub-app or adds a proxy-header
  middleware; `cli.py:225` is a plain `uvicorn.run`. So the door now decides on
  exactly the path the router serves.
  - Any case where the two could still differ fails closed: a `/healthz/` path,
  or a root_path set later, would ask for sign-in rather than open.
  - The new comment is accurate. Starlette 1.10.0 (uv.lock:462) rebuilds the URL
  with the Host header only after `parse_host_header` accepts it
  (datastructures.py:48-50), and that check's regex rejects `/`, `?` and `#`.

  **Finding P2-1 (minor, high confidence, informational):**
  tests/test_public.py:78-85
  - On the locked Starlette 1.10.0 this test passes against the old
  `request.url.path` code too, because Starlette's own Host check already blocks
  the attack.
  - So it cannot go red first. It is a tripwire that fails only if a Starlette
  upgrade drops that check and the door is back on the URL path. That is exactly
  the owner's "so a dependency change cannot open the door", so this is not a
  defect.
  - But it is not proof of the fix. Nothing that leaves the machine should
  describe it that way.

  **Finding P2-2 (minor, high confidence):** tests/test_public.py:78
  - The owner asked for one test with `Host: x/healthz?`. It is one test
  function run over three hosts.
  - `"x/healthz#"` is a real second attack (the fragment hides the rest of the
  path).
  - `"x/healthz"` can never open the door: the rebuilt path would be
  `/healthz/api/hubs`, which is not an open path. It costs little, but it tests
  nothing. Drop it or keep it, either is fine.

  **Other points, no action needed:**
  - `model.requests == []` asserts on the scripted fake model, but it backs the
  real status check (401) and copies the existing test at line 49. It is not a
  mock-only assertion.
  - Starlette's TestClient passes a caller-supplied Host header through to the
  server, so the test really does send the forged header.

  **Verdict:** does the task, stays in scope apart from the extra cases in P2-2,
  fits demo grade.

  ## Part 2(b): Koyeb to Render comments (Dockerfile:1, :9;
  src/wra/agent/limits.py:4-6)

  **The comments are accurate.**
  - DEPLOY_DESIGN.md section 9 exists and replaces the Koyeb parts of 5.6, so
  "5.6 and 9" is the right pointer.
  - "Byte-compile the dependencies" is accurate: the project is installed
  editable, so `UV_COMPILE_BYTECODE` compiles only site-packages.
  - The restart causes in limits.py ("a deploy or a crash") match the project

  **Finding P2-3 (minor, low confidence):** Dockerfile:1

  Small wording gap; optional fix.

  **Finding P2-4 (minor, high confidence, outside the diff):**
  /Users/doronreiffman/Projects/weather-risk-agent/README.md:69
  - The interview-day runbook still says "open the public URL so it wakes".
  DEPLOY_DESIGN.md section 9 says the paid Render instance never sleeps and the

  **Verdict:** the comment changes do the task, stay in scope and fit demo
